      "text": "[\n  {\n    \"index\": 23,\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/SKILL.md\",\n    \"content\": \"---\\nname: interrogate\\ndescription: \\\"Use for \\\\\\\"interrogate\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"adversarial review\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"multi-model review\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"challenge this\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"stress test this code\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"find blind spots\\\\\\\", or \\\\\\\"tear this apart\\\\\\\". Multiple LLM reviewers challenge changes from independent angles.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Interrogate\\n\\nSpawn one reviewer per configured model to adversarially review code changes. Each model gets the same prompt and rubric. The adversarial signal comes from model diversity, not assigned personas.\\n\\nThe deliverable is a synthesized verdict. Do NOT auto-apply changes.\\n\\n## Step 1, Determine Scope\\n\\nIdentify what to review from context:\\n\\n- If the user points at specific files or a diff, use that\\n- If on a feature branch, run `git diff main...HEAD` (or the appropriate base branch) for the full changeset\\n- If the user's message references recent work, gather the relevant files\\n\\nPackage the diff (or file contents) plus any surrounding context files the reviewers need to understand the code.\\n\\n## Step 2, State the Intent\\n\\nBefore spawning reviewers, state the intent explicitly. Derive this from:\\n\\n- The user's message\\n- Commit messages\\n- PR description if one exists\\n- The code itself\\n\\nWrite one clear paragraph. If you're unsure about the intent, ask the user before proceeding.\\n\\n## Step 3, Spawn Reviewers\\n\\nUse [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model selection](../../references/model-roles.md), and the [assignment contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md). Launch authorized independent read-only reviewers together through the exposed worker operation, one per explicitly configured reviewer selection. Extend or shrink Reviewer A/B labels to the selection count. Give each an immutable assignment, the same input revision and separate result ownership. Inspect actual results before synthesis.\\n\\nThe canonical source used a local model rule and default requests from the Claude and Grok families. The exact historical defaults are preserved as provenance in the [adaptation record](../../docs/parity/adaptations/interrogate.json). These historical requests do not establish available models. Preserve explicit session selections. With no configured selections, request two inherited reviewers and disclose that inherited or identical models do not establish model diversity. For auto or inherit-parent, omit explicit model selection. Never treat aliases as rejected model names.\\n\\nSelect explicit models and effort only from current exposed tool support. If a configured request is rejected, disclose it and use an authorized supported alternative, preferring the same family and reasoning tier where available. If the exact selection is required, report blocked. Record requested and observed models separately; missing observations remain unknown. Do not open a PR or change settings to repair upstream defaults without explicit authorization.\\n\\nWhen independent workers are unavailable, apply the same prompt and rubric sequentially with authorization and label reduced capability. Do not call repeated passes independent reviewers or consensus. Missing observed model diversity limits that claim even when worker independence succeeds. Denied review or missing source blocks grounded findings. Do not install tools or configure agents as an incidental step.\\n\\nRead `references/reviewer-prompt.md` and fill in the template with:\\n1. The stated intent\\n2. The diff or file contents\\n3. The review rubric from `references/rubric.md`\\n4. The code-quality lens from `references/code-quality-review.md`\\n\\nThe same filled template goes to all reviewers, so every model applies the code-quality lens.\\n\\n## Step 4, Synthesize\\n\\nAs results come back, build a unified picture:\\n\\n1. **Parse all findings** from the reviewers\\n2. **Identify consensus**. Findings raised by 2+ models independently are highest signal.\\n3. **Identify lone-model findings**. Still worth reading, but weight accordingly.\\n4. **Deduplicate**. Different models may describe the same issue differently. Merge these and note which models raised it.\\n5. **Note disagreements**. If one model flags something and another explicitly says the opposite, that's useful context for the verdict.\\n\\n## Step 5, Lead Judgment\\n\\nYou are the lead reviewer, a pragmatic senior engineer, not a neutral aggregator.\\n\\nRead `references/lead-judgment.md` for the full framework.\\n\\nCategorize every finding using these buckets:\\n\\n- **Act on**. Real issues affecting correctness, security, or maintainability given the actual goals. These would block a real PR.\\n- **Consider**. Legitimate points, but you're not sure they outweigh the cost of addressing them right now. Worth the user's attention.\\n- **Noted**. Technically valid but not actionable. Context-dependent, premature optimization, or low-impact given the current stage.\\n- **Dismissed**. Wrong, nitpicky, or missing context. Brief explanation why.\\n\\nFor each finding, include:\\n- Which model(s) raised it\\n- The category (act on / consider / noted / dismissed)\\n- A one-line rationale for the categorization\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nPresent the verdict in this structure:\\n\\n### Intent\\n> [The stated intent paragraph from Step 2]\\n\\n### Reviewers\\nReport requested and observed models, worker independence, model diversity and capability limits. Missing observations remain unknown.\\n\\n- Reviewer [label]: [model name], [N findings] (one bullet per reviewer)\\n\\n### Act On\\n[Findings that should be addressed. For each: description, which models raised it, why it matters.]\\n\\n### Consider\\n[Findings worth thinking about. For each: description, which models raised it, tradeoff involved.]\\n\\n### Noted\\n[Valid but low-priority. Brief list.]\\n\\n### Dismissed\\n[Rejected findings with brief rationale.]\\n\\n### Agreement Map\\n[Where did models agree, where did they diverge, and what does the pattern of agreement/disagreement tell us?]\\n\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"index\": 24,\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/reviewer-prompt.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Reviewer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. Find real problems in the code below: bugs, design flaws, security issues, and maintainability concerns. You are not here to be helpful or encouraging. You are here to stress-test.\\n\\n## Intent\\n\\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\\n\\n> {INTENT}\\n\\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\\n\\n## Code Under Review\\n\\n{DIFF_OR_FILES}\\n\\n## Review Rubric\\n\\n{RUBRIC_CONTENTS}\\n\\n## Code Quality Lens\\n\\n{CODE_QUALITY_CONTENTS}\\n\\n## Instructions\\n\\nReview the code through every lens in the rubric and the code-quality lens above that you find relevant. Do not force lenses that don't apply. A simple bug fix does not need paragraphs about architectural integrity.\\n\\nFor each finding, provide:\\n\\n1. **Severity**: `critical` | `warning` | `nit`\\n   - `critical`: Would cause bugs, data loss, security issues, or fundamentally broken behavior\\n   - `warning`: Design concern, maintainability risk, or correctness issue that isn't immediately broken but will cause pain\\n   - `nit`: Style, naming, minor improvement.\\n2. **Finding**: What the problem is, in concrete terms. Reference specific lines/functions.\\n3. **Evidence**: Why you believe this is a problem. Show your reasoning. Don't just assert.\\n4. **Suggestion** (optional): What you'd do instead, if you have a concrete alternative. Skip this if you don't have a clear fix.\\n\\n## What Makes a Good Finding\\n\\n- It references specific code, not vague concerns (\\\"this could be better\\\")\\n- It explains WHY something is a problem, not just THAT it is\\n- It distinguishes between \\\"this is broken\\\" and \\\"I would have done this differently\\\"\\n- It considers the stated intent. A finding that ignores the context of what's being built is a bad finding\\n\\n## What to Avoid\\n\\n- Restating what the code does without identifying a problem\\n- Praising the code. You're an adversary, not a cheerleader. If you find nothing wrong, say \\\"no findings\\\" and stop.\\n\\n## Output\\n\\nReturn your findings as a structured list. If you have zero findings, say so. An empty review is a valid outcome.\\n\\n```\\n## Findings\\n\\n### 1. [Severity] Short title\\n**Location**: file:line or function name\\n**Finding**: What's wrong\\n**Evidence**: Why this matters\\n**Suggestion**: (optional) What to do instead\\n\\n### 2. [Severity] Short title\\n...\\n```\\n\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"index\": 25,\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/rubric.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Review Rubric\\n\\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\\n\\n## Correctness\\n\\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\\n\\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \\\"it depends on what state was left behind,\\\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\\n\\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \\\"this could be nil\\\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\\n\\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\\n\\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\\n\\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\\n\\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? What would a proper fix look like?\\n- A fix in module A that should really be a fix in module B's contract\\n- Instructions where structure would be better: if the fix is a comment saying \\\"don't do X\\\" or a convention someone has to remember, ask whether it could instead be a type constraint, a lint rule, or a runtime check that makes the wrong thing impossible\\n\\n## Structural Integrity\\n\\nDoes the code fit well into the system it's part of?\\n\\n- Boundary discipline: is validation at system boundaries, or scattered through business logic? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? If there are no external consumers, migrate callers and delete the old path in the same wave. Don't leave compatibility layers that will become permanent.\\n\\nDon't penalize simple code for lacking abstraction. Premature abstraction is worse than duplication.\\n\\n## Verification\\n\\nCan you tell that this code works from reading it?\\n\\n- Are there tests? Do they test behavior or implementation details?\\n- Are there assertions/invariants that would catch regressions?\\n- If this is a bug fix: is there a test for the bug?\\n- If this touches an integration boundary: is the full path tested?\\n- Check the real thing, not a proxy. If the code checks liveness via file mtime or cached state instead of reading the actual value, that's a verification gap.\\n- For delegated or async work: does the code verify actual output artifacts, or does it trust self-reports and summaries?\\n\\n## Complexity Budget\\n\\nIs the complexity justified by what the code accomplishes?\\n\\n- Code that could be simpler without losing correctness or clarity\\n- Abstractions that serve only one call site\\n- Configuration or parameterization for cases that don't exist yet\\n- Dead code, unused imports, vestigial parameters\\n- Over-engineering: \\\"just in case\\\" code paths with no current callers\\n- Obsolete compatibility paths kept alive for transitional stability that's no longer needed. If the migration is done, delete the scaffolding\\n- Does the user experience justify the complexity? Every feature, control, and option should earn its place. Half-finished features are worse than missing ones.\\n\\nSimpler is better unless simpler is wrong. Three lines of duplication beat a premature abstraction.\\n\\n## Security\\n\\nFor each security finding, trace the input path through the code and show it.\\n\\n- User input flowing to dangerous sinks (SQL, shell, eval, innerHTML) without sanitization\\n- Authentication/authorization gaps in new endpoints\\n- Secrets in code, logs, or error messages\\n- TOCTOU (time-of-check-time-of-use) in security-critical paths\\n\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"index\": 26,\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/code-quality-review.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Code Quality Review\\n\\nEach reviewer applies this code-quality lens in addition to the rubric. It is a strict standard focused on implementation quality, maintainability, abstraction quality, and codebase health.\\n\\nAbove all, be ambitious about code structure. Do not merely identify local cleanup. Actively search for \\\"code judo\\\" moves, restructurings that preserve behavior while making the implementation dramatically simpler, smaller, more direct, and more elegant.\\n\\n## Core Prompt\\n\\nStart from this baseline:\\n\\n> Perform a deep code quality audit of the current branch's changes.\\n> Rethink how to structure / implement the changes to meaningfully improve code quality without impacting behavior.\\n> Work to improve abstractions, modularity, reduce Spaghetti code, improve succinctness and legibility.\\n> Be ambitious, if there is a clear path to improving the implementation that involves restructuring some of the codebase, go for it.\\n> Be extremely thorough and rigorous. Measure twice, cut once.\\n\\n## Dimensions\\n\\nEach dimension is stated once. Apply the ones that are relevant.\\n\\n0. **Be ambitious about structural simplification.** Do not stop at \\\"this could be a bit cleaner.\\\" Look for reframings that make whole branches, helpers, modes, conditionals, or layers disappear. Assume a \\\"code judo\\\" move is often available. It uses the existing architecture more effectively and makes the change dramatically simpler. If you can delete complexity rather than rearrange it, push hard for that.\\n\\n1. **Do not let a PR push a file from under 1k lines to over 1k lines without a very strong reason.** Treat this as a strong smell. Prefer extracting helpers, subcomponents, or modules. If the diff crosses that threshold, ask whether the code should be decomposed first. Waive only for a compelling structural reason where the resulting file stays clearly organized.\\n\\n2. **Do not allow spaghetti growth in existing code.** Be suspicious of new ad-hoc conditionals, scattered special cases, or one-off branches inserted into unrelated flows. Treat \\\"weird if statements in random places\\\" as a design problem, not a style nit. Prefer pushing the logic into a dedicated helper, state machine, or module instead of tangling an existing path.\\n\\n3. **Bias toward cleaning the design, not just accepting working code.** If behavior can stay the same while the structure becomes meaningfully cleaner, push for the cleaner version. Prefer simplifications that remove moving pieces over refactors that spread the same complexity around.\\n\\n4. **Prefer direct, boring, maintainable code over hacky or magical code.** Treat brittle, ad-hoc, or \\\"magic\\\" behavior as a problem. Be skeptical of generic mechanisms that hide simple data-shape assumptions. Flag thin abstractions, identity wrappers, or pass-through helpers that add indirection without buying clarity.\\n\\n5. **Push on type and boundary cleanliness when it affects maintainability.** Question unnecessary optionality, `unknown`, `any`, or cast-heavy code when a clearer type boundary could exist. Prefer explicit typed models over loosely-shaped ad-hoc objects. If a branch leans on a silent fallback to paper over an unclear invariant, ask whether the boundary should be made explicit.\\n\\n6. **Keep logic in the canonical layer and reuse existing helpers.** Call out feature logic leaking into shared paths or implementation details leaking through APIs. Prefer existing canonical utilities over bespoke one-offs. Push code toward the right package, service, or module instead of normalizing drift.\\n\\n7. **Treat unnecessary sequential orchestration and non-atomic updates as design smells when the cleaner structure is obvious.** If independent work is serialized for no reason, ask whether it should run in parallel. If related updates can leave state half-applied, push for a more atomic structure. Do not over-index on micro-optimizations, but do flag avoidable orchestration complexity that makes the code more brittle.\\n\\n## Output Expectations\\n\\nPrioritize structural code-quality regressions and missed simplifications first, then spaghetti and branching complexity, then boundary, type, and file-size concerns, then smaller modularity and legibility issues.\\n\\n## Approval Bar\\n\\nDo not approve merely because behavior seems correct. Treat these as presumptive blockers unless the author can justify them: the PR keeps a lot of incidental complexity when a code-judo move would delete it. Pushes a file from below 1000 lines to above 1000 lines. Adds ad-hoc branching that tangles an existing flow. Scatters feature checks across shared code. Adds an unnecessary abstraction, wrapper, or cast-heavy contract, or duplicates an existing helper or puts logic in the wrong layer when there is a clear canonical home. If those conditions are not met, leave explicit, actionable feedback and push for a cleaner decomposition.\\n\\n## Review Tone\\n\\nBe direct, serious, and demanding about quality. Do not be rude, but do not soften major maintainability issues into mild suggestions. If the code is making the codebase messier, say so. If the implementation missed an obvious dramatic simplification, say that too. Do not be satisfied with \\\"maybe rename this\\\" when the real issue is structural.\\n\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"index\": 27,\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/lead-judgment.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Lead Judgment Framework\\n\\nYou are the lead reviewer. The configured reviewers have produced their findings. Apply pragmatic engineering judgment. Don't aggregate. Filter, contextualize, and decide.\\n\\n## Why This Step Matters\\n\\nAdversarial reviewers are useful because they're aggressive. But aggression without context produces noise. The reviewers only saw a slice of the codebase and a one-paragraph intent statement. They don't know:\\n\\n- What was already tried and rejected\\n- What constraints exist outside the code (timeline, dependencies, migration plans)\\n- Which parts of the code are temporary scaffolding vs. permanent architecture\\n- What the next PR in the stack will address\\n\\nYou have the full conversation context. Use it.\\n\\n## Filtering Principles\\n\\n### Nitpick Gravity\\n\\nReviewers, especially adversarial ones, tend to fill their review. If they don't find critical issues, they'll inflate nits to fill the space. If a reviewer's findings are all nits and style preferences, the code is probably fine. Say so.\\n\\n### Hypothetical vs. Actual\\n\\n\\\"What if someone passes null here?\\\" is only a finding if the caller can actually pass null. Trace the call site. If the input is validated upstream or the type system prevents it, dismiss the finding. Reviewers working from a diff can't always see the full call chain. You can.\\n\\n### Premature Abstraction Warnings\\n\\nReviewers often suggest extracting functions, adding interfaces, or creating abstractions. Does this code need to change in a second way? If not, the abstraction is premature. Simple inline code that works beats a clean abstraction that's overkill for the current scope.\\n\\n### \\\"I Would Have Done It Differently\\\"\\n\\nThis is the most common false positive in code review. A finding that amounts to \\\"I prefer a different approach\\\" is not a bug, not a design flaw, and not actionable unless the reviewer shows a concrete problem with the current approach. Dismiss these, and say why.\\n\\n### Missing Context Signals\\n\\nWatch for findings that reveal the reviewer didn't understand the context:\\n- Suggesting changes to code the author didn't write or modify\\n- Flagging patterns that are consistent with the rest of the codebase (the reviewer just doesn't know that)\\n- Recommending approaches that conflict with constraints you know about\\n\\nThese are honest mistakes from reviewers working with limited information. Dismiss them gracefully.\\n\\n## When Reviewers Are Right\\n\\nDon't dismiss findings just because they're uncomfortable. The whole point of adversarial review is to catch things you'd miss. Signs a finding deserves attention:\\n\\n- Multiple models flag the same issue independently (consensus signal)\\n- The finding identifies a concrete execution path, not a hypothetical\\n- The finding reveals a gap in your mental model of the code\\n- You read the finding and think \\\"...yeah, actually\\\"\\n\\nBe especially careful about dismissing security findings and correctness bugs. These deserve more scrutiny even when they come from a single model.\\n\\n## Verdict Calibration\\n\\nA good verdict is useful, not comprehensive. The user should be able to read the \\\"Act On\\\" section, fix those issues, and ship with confidence. If your \\\"Act On\\\" list has more than 5 items, you're probably not filtering hard enough.\\n\\nThe \\\"Dismissed\\\" section is not busywork. It's a trust mechanism. Showing the user what you rejected and why lets them override your judgment where they disagree. This is more valuable than hiding the rejected findings.\\n\"\n  }\n]"

      "text": "{\n  \"id\": \"bug_synth-1\",\n  \"scope\": {\n    \"reads\": [\n      \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-013-20261016/fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/outputs/bug_why-capture/prior-return.txt\",\n      \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-013-20261016/fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/outputs/current-state-and-digest.raw.json\",\n      \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-013-20261016/fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/outputs/local-grounding.raw.json\"\n    ],\n    \"writes\": []\n  },\n  \"inputRevision\": \"89e929f4f50ae93d5721503acc51261a260b6b4b\",\n  \"outputOwner\": \"/root/bug_synth\",\n  \"isolatedOutputPath\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-013-20261016/fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/outputs/bug_synth-return.txt\",\n  \"artifactCaptureOwner\": \"/root\",\n  \"producingWorker\": \"/root/bug_synth\",\n  \"role\": \"why synthesizer\",\n  \"retryCount\": 0,\n  \"terminalStatus\": \"pending\",\n  \"evidenceLinks\": [\n    \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-013-20261016/fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/outputs/bug_why-capture/prior-return.txt\"\n  ],\n  \"requestedModel\": \"inherit-parent\",\n  \"actualModel\": \"unknown\"\n}"

    {
      "path": "fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/outputs/source-group-28-31.raw.json",
      "bytes": 15620,
      "sha256": "9b96ad84c58e76f82c3c690040249bf7d266ee4b5889aebf636d16992e39b82b",
      "owner": "local-013-initial-reproduce-before-fix",
      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "[\n  {\n    \"index\": 28,\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/tdd/SKILL.md\",\n    \"content\": \"---\\nname: tdd\\ndescription: \\\"Use only when the user explicitly asks for TDD, a failing test, or a regression test, OR when the bug has an obvious cheap local test target. Skip when the test path is unclear, expensive, integration-heavy, or not requested.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# TDD Bug Fix\\n\\nWhen fixing a bug with a clear, cheap test path, make the broken behavior executable before changing production code. The goal is a focused regression test that fails before the fix and passes after it.\\n\\nDo not force a test when it would be impractical. If the available test would require broad harness setup, brittle mocks, slow end-to-end infrastructure, production-only state, vague reproduction steps, or large unrelated fixture churn, skip adding a new test and use the closest useful verification instead.\\n\\n## Workflow\\n\\n1. **Understand the bug.** Identify the intended behavior, current behavior, affected path, and smallest observable reproduction.\\n2. **Choose the narrowest executable check.** Prefer the closest unit, component, integration, or regression test already used for that codepath. If no practical test path is obvious, do not create one from scratch just to satisfy the workflow.\\n3. **Write the failing test first.** Add the smallest focused test that would have caught the bug. The test should encode intended behavior, not mirror the current implementation.\\n4. **Run the new test before fixing.** Confirm it fails for the intended reason. If it passes or fails for an unrelated reason, correct the test or reproduction before editing the implementation.\\n5. **Fix the bug.** Make the smallest production change that satisfies the intended behavior while preserving nearby contracts.\\n6. **Rerun the regression test.** Confirm the test now passes.\\n\\n## If a Failing Test Is Impractical\\n\\nUse the closest executable regression check instead: a targeted script, manual reproduction command, browser automation, snapshot comparison, log assertion, or focused integration check.\\n\\nPrefer no new test over a bad test. A bad test is one that mostly tests mocks, encodes current implementation details, depends on timing or unrelated global state, needs expensive infrastructure for a small fix, or would be deleted immediately after proving the fix.\\n\\n## Guardrails\\n\\n- Do not change tests merely to match a wrong implementation.\\n- Do not weaken existing assertions unless the expected behavior has genuinely changed and the reason is clear.\\n- Keep the regression test focused on the bug. Avoid broad fixture churn or unrelated coverage expansion.\\n- If the bug is flaky, make the test deterministic where possible and document the signal being locked down.\\n- If the bug exposes a broader class of failures, first land the focused regression path, then consider additional sibling coverage.\\n\\n## Final Response\\n\\nReport the evidence, not just the outcome:\\n\\n- Name the failing-before test or executable check and the failure it produced.\\n- Name the passing-after test run and any nearby validation performed.\\n- If failing-before evidence could not be demonstrated, state why and describe the closest regression check used instead.\\n\\n## Session execution limits\\n\\nBind file access, writes, and execution to the tools actually exposed and authorized in this session. Follow [the capability contract](../../references/host-capabilities.md). An exposed execution tool does not prove a test ran. Keep the command, exit status, and output for each observed run.\\n\\nIf execution is unavailable or denied, describe the proposed failing-before and passing-after sequence as unrun. State the missing capability and the available verification. Do not claim a failing test, passing test, or demonstrated regression fix without execution evidence.\\n\\nRetain the explicit invocation metadata above without assuming the host enforces it.\\n\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"index\": 29,\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/principle-sequence-verifiable-units/SKILL.md\",\n    \"content\": \"---\\nname: principle-sequence-verifiable-units\\ndescription: \\\"Apply to multi-step work (sweeps, migrations, runs of similar edits) and to how you stack commits and PRs. Break work into small units that each end in a verifiable state, check each before the next, and order delivery so the sequence proves itself to a reviewer.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Sequence work into verifiable units\\n\\nOrder work as a sequence of small units, each ending in a state you can check, and don't advance until the current one is green.\\n\\n**Why:** A break caught at the unit that caused it is cheap to localize. A break caught after a batch is buried, and you have already built further on a broken base. Sequencing those same units into a delivery a reviewer can replay turns \\\"trust me\\\" into \\\"watch it go red, then green.\\\"\\n\\n**Execution.** In a sweep, migration, or any run of similar edits, verify each change before starting the next. Each unit is a before/after bracket: known-good state, one change, run the check, then proceed. Rebase onto clean trunk first so every check measures against the real baseline. When a lever does the edits, the per-unit check is nearly free. Run it anyway.\\n\\n**Delivery.** Stack commits and PRs in the order that proves the work. The canonical shape is the failing test first, then the fix on top. Other story orders are a subtraction before the reshape, a baseline capture before the treatment, the scaffold before the feature. Each commit lands on its own and the sequence reads as an argument.\\n\\nThe sequencing complement to the **prove-it-works** principle skill, which keeps each check real, and the **build-the-lever** principle skill, which makes the per-unit check cheap.\\n\\n## Capability and authorization boundary\\n\\nUse [host capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md) to establish available inspection and execution, and the [orchestration contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md) for write ownership and authorization. Rebase onto clean trunk only when authorized and safe for the current work. Commits and PRs also require authorization and an available operation. If a required check cannot run, state that it remains unverified and block advancement; an unrun check is never green. A reduced workflow may provide the ordered units, specific checks and delivery plan while identifying unperformed operations. Preserve `disable-model-invocation` metadata without assuming host enforcement.\\n\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"index\": 30,\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/opening-a-pr.md\",\n    \"content\": \"### Opening a PR\\n\\nInvoked at the end of every other playbook.\\n\\nUse [session capabilities](../../../references/host-capabilities.md), [workflow ownership](../../../references/workflow-ownership.md), [model roles](../../../references/model-roles.md), and the [assignment contract](../../../references/orchestration-contract.md). These documents live at the package root. Prepare a reviewable title, body, verification artifacts, and intended base before any external operation. Record explicit authorization, the repository, branch head, destination and action identity. Check the destination after an interrupted action before any replay. Missing access or permission leaves the external action blocked and the content available locally. Draft-only permission permits a draft, never readiness, push, merge or comments.\\n\\n**Worktree.** Work from a git worktree off main. Subagents inherit it. Multiple independent workers on the same branch each get their own isolated worktree. Preserve unrelated dirty work before creating an authorized fresh worktree. Report a snarled worktree instead of resetting or discarding unowned changes. Missing independent workers permits an authorized sequential reduction, never a claim of independent review.\\n\\n**Commits.** Commit liberally within the authorized local scope. Rebase into small, ordered commits before opening PRs. Each commit is a future PR: landable, ordered to tell the story. Amend when the fix belongs in a just-made commit. New commit when separable.\\n\\n**PRs.** Run `/deslop` over the diff before commit if that external skill is exposed and authorized. It is not bundled here. If absent, disclose the missing slop-strip and leave that gate open and block creation until the required review gate is satisfied; do not install it or treat prose cleanup as equivalent. Run `/no-comments` through [no-comments](../../no-comments/SKILL.md) before review. Write every PR title, PR description, and commit body with `/technical-writing` through [technical-writing](../../technical-writing/SKILL.md), then apply `/unslop` through [unslop](../../unslop/SKILL.md). Apply every technical-writing layer except Diátaxis. Use one word for each action, keep articles, and avoid `-ing` when a plain verb works.\\n\\n**Titles.** Use Conventional Commits in the form `type(scope): subject`. Use `feat`, `fix`, `docs`, `refactor`, `test`, `chore`, or `perf` as the type. Use the changed area, such as `pstack` or `poteto-mode`, as the scope. Keep the subject short and imperative. Name a real symbol when one carries the change. For example, `fix(pstack): retarget opening-a-pr babysit trigger`. Do not add a trailing period.\\n\\n**Descriptions.** The PR body is a briefing, not the lab notebook. A reviewer who has the diff should learn why the change exists, what it leaves out, what it could break, and how you proved it works, in under a minute. Write short, simple sentences with few identifiers. Do not write walls of text. The squash commit body is the PR body. If the body would make the squash commit longer than about 40 lines, cut the body.\\n\\nPut each section under a `##` heading, not a bold lead-in, so the sections stand apart. Use these sections in order. Drop a section when it has nothing to say.\\n\\n- `## Why` gives the problem and the approach in one to three short sentences. Do not list SHAs or rebase genealogy. Do not add a \\\"based on main\\\" preamble.\\n- `## What changed` has one to three short bullets. Name a real symbol or path only when it carries the change. Name both sides of a rename or retarget.\\n- `## Scope` always names what the PR covers and what it deliberately leaves out, for example a related follow-up or a known gap. Use one to three short items. Do not list symbols or paths, and do not write a file-by-file essay.\\n- `## Tradeoffs` names only rejected alternatives that a reviewer would otherwise ask about. Skip this section when there was no real choice.\\n- `## Blast Radius` gives one or two sentences on who or what the change touches and why that is safe or risky. If main is red, state the cost of leaving it red.\\n- `## Verification` has one to three bullets. Each bullet names a real run path and its outcome. For a performance change, report one primary number with its unit in `before → after` form. Link the arena or swarm directory for the remaining evidence. Do not include sample-size methodology, swarm recitals, or metric tables.\\n\\nAfter these sections, attach videos or screenshots when they prove a claim. Do not paste full SHAs, swarm or arena lane recitals, lever-correction essays, file-by-file checklists, or \\\"CLEAN\\\" verdicts. Put these details in a linked artifact. A commit body does not restate its subject.\\n\\n**Forge.** Resolve the forge before the first PR operation and keep that choice for create, edit, view, watch, and merge. GitHub CLI (`gh`) is the default. If `command -v origin` succeeds and Origin can resolve the repository, prefer `origin pr ...`. If Origin is absent or cannot resolve the repository, stay on `gh` and record the fallback. Do not require Graphite (`gt`).\\n\\n**Built-in PR tool.** When the run provides a built-in PR tool, create, edit, retarget, and mark ready through it, never through a forge CLI. Its exposed instructions define the actual interface. An unavailable, denied or failed tool is a stated blocker, not permission to bypass its tracking through a CLI. A PR made with the CLI misses what the tool tracks, such as a description later runs can edit. Use the resolved forge for everything the tool does not cover, and for every PR operation when the run has no such tool.\\n\\n**Size and stacks.** Prefer five narrow PRs to one large PR. A stack is a base-branch chain. The root PR targets trunk. Each child branch rebases onto its parent's exact tip and its PR targets the parent branch. Without a built-in PR tool, create a child with `origin pr create --status open --base <parent-branch>` or `gh pr create --base <parent-branch>` according to the resolved forge, and retarget an existing child with `origin pr edit <pr> --base <parent-branch>` or `gh pr edit <pr> --base <parent-branch>`. Branch from trunk only for independent work. Rebase on trunk before substantial stack work.\\n\\n**Readiness.** The source workflow opens every PR ready, never as a draft, when readiness is explicitly authorized. If only draft creation is authorized, preserve that limit and create a draft through the exposed tool or `gh pr create --draft`. Verify the actual draft state and report the readiness gate open. Use Origin draft creation only when its current interface supports it. Otherwise report blocked. All readiness instructions below require readiness authorization. A built-in PR tool can default to draft, so use its observed ready-creation interface, such as `draft: false` only when that field is actually supported. With Origin, pass `--status open`. With `gh`, omit `--draft`. If a PR still opens as a draft, mark it ready through the PR tool, or run `origin pr ready <number>` or `gh pr ready <number>` according to the resolved forge. Run `origin pr view <number>` or `gh pr view <number>` before you refer to PR status.\\n\\n**Babysit.** Opening a PR does not start a babysit. Post the URL and keep building. Finish the phase or stack first. Run a separate babysit pass only when the user asks for one after the whole stack exists. A babysit for each new PR stalls the build and spends checks on commits that later waves restart. Push back when feedback drifts from intent.\\n\\nA subagent that opens a PR runs `interrogate` through [interrogate](../../interrogate/SKILL.md), `/deslop`, and `/no-comments`, and posts the URL. Then it returns to the parent without babysitting, unless it is an Autopilot-full or Autopilot-stack owner. That owner's brief assigns the babysit loop and is the ask [babysit](babysit.md) waits for. The owner starts the loop after its code-ready report and reports merge-ready or STACK-READY as its playbook says. The rules here and in [babysit](babysit.md) that hold babysitting until a whole stack is built do not apply to that owner.\\n\\nFor the owner exception, read [Autopilot-full](autopilot-full.md) or [Autopilot-stack](autopilot-stack.md) and the [Bugbot triage rubric](../references/bugbot-triage.md). These briefs assign babysitting only within explicit user authorization and observed capabilities. Opening a PR alone never supplies that authorization. No watcher, bootstrap or orchestration helper runs in this opening step. Babysit owns the actual watcher binding.\\n\"\n  }\n]"
    },

      "path": "fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/outputs/bug_how-capture/prior-return.txt",

      "sha256": "e520d83f6e55729be83fa0a0319c315322afd0a8306e7ff02bea8da967fca250",

      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "### Overview\n\nThe inspected dashboard module has no entry point for the reported search-clear stale-row trigger. It exports a status filter and a CSV formatter. There is no search control, query state, clear handler, browser rendering, or cached row state in the supplied module.\n\nAssignment `bug-how-1`; producing worker and output owner `/root/bug_how`; input revision `89e929f4f50ae93d5721503acc51261a260b6b4b`. Full HEAD matches that revision. Requested model: `inherit-parent`; actual model and effort: unknown. This was a direct, read-only explainer investigation with no nested delegation or file writes. `/root` captures the returned text.\n\n### Key Concepts\n\n- `visible(orders, status)` selects orders whose `status` strictly equals the supplied status.\n- `exportCsv(orders)` serializes the supplied orders into three columns: `id`, `customer`, and `total`.\n- `runtime.mjs` is a separate synthetic computation that builds records, sums keys, and logs a result. It contains no dashboard integration.\n\n### How It Works\n\nA caller supplies an order array and status to `visible()`. The function immediately calls `orders.filter()` and returns the matching array. It retains no state between calls. Its only selection criterion is `o.status === status`; it does not inspect a search string.\n\nA caller can separately pass an array to `exportCsv()`. The formatter prepends a fixed header and joins each order’s fields with commas. It does not call `visible()` or maintain the displayed rows. Any relationship between filtering, displayed rows, and export would have to be implemented by a caller outside the inspected module.\n\nThe missing mechanism is the connection from a browser search control through query changes and clearing to row recomputation and rendering. None of those stages appears in the assigned dashboard source. Exercising its status filter would therefore not reproduce the reported browser-search sequence.\n\n### Where Things Live\n\nAll paths are under `/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-013-20261016/fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/`.\n\n| Path | Relevant contents |\n| --- | --- |\n| `committed-inputs/shared/dashboard.mjs:1` | Stateless status filter |\n| `committed-inputs/shared/dashboard.mjs:2` | CSV formatter |\n| `committed-inputs/shared/runtime.mjs:1` | Separate synthetic computation |\n| `committed-inputs/shared/history.json` | Explicitly inert fixture history |\n| `outputs/local-grounding.raw.json` | Supplied local command observations |\n\n### Gotchas\n\nBrowser reproduction remains blocked by the supplied locked-Mac and missing-browser-inventory limitation. I did not inspect a browser or attempt service access. This source investigation neither reproduces nor disproves the reported production behavior. A runnable dashboard with its search control, event handlers, row-rendering path, and reproduction inputs is needed to trace that trigger.\n\nThe supplied history records a migration proposal, not a shipped change. It provides no evidence about search behavior.\n\nObserved commands and results:\n\n| Command | Observed output | Exit |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `git rev-parse HEAD` | `89e929f4f50ae93d5721503acc51261a260b6b4b` | 0, combined read command |\n| `git status --short` | `?? outputs/` | 0, combined read command |\n| `git diff --exit-code HEAD -- committed-inputs/shared/dashboard.mjs committed-inputs/shared/runtime.mjs committed-inputs/shared/history.json` | Empty output; assigned tracked sources match HEAD | 0 |\n| `rg -n 'search\\|query\\|filter\\|input\\|cache\\|export\\|visible' committed-inputs/shared/dashboard.mjs` | Only the two exported functions above | 0 |\n\nThe complete frozen `how/SKILL.md`, explainer template, filled task prompt, assignment record, and required frozen support references were read before source tracing. Those read commands completed with exit 0. No failing or passing browser reproduction result was observed or claimed."

      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "# Investigator Prompt Template\n\nBuild each investigator's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders. Append the single category playbook `sources/<source>.md` matching this investigator's evidence category (see `source-playbook.md` for the index). If the target code looks defensive (null checks, retry logic, timeout handling, rate limiting, feature flags, egress guards, OOM handlers), also append `sources/incident-postmortem.md` for the incident-flavored queries to run inside its own source.\n\n---\n\nYou are investigating the historical context and motivation behind a piece of code. A separate synthesizer combines your findings with other investigators' into a final answer, so gather evidence accurately rather than writing prose.\n\nOther investigators search different sources in parallel. Don't try to cover everything. Focus on your assigned source and go deep.\n\n## Operating Posture\n\nWork like a careful, cautious, precise investigator. Don't produce a narrative. Surface evidence and describe it accurately, including the parts that don't fit a tidy story. The more boring and exact your output, the more useful it is. A single verbatim quote with a precise citation beats a paragraph of plausible-sounding summary.\n\n- **Quote, don't paraphrase** when the exact wording matters. Citations should let the reader jump to the source and confirm the claim in seconds.\n- **Go wide before going deep.** Cast a broad first net so you don't miss related context. Only then narrow in.\n- **Track what you searched, not just what you found.** An absence is only useful if the reader knows what was looked for. Record queries verbatim.\n- **Resist the story.** If three pieces of evidence line up neatly and a fourth contradicts them, the contradiction is the most interesting finding. Don't file it away.\n- **Consider the counterfactual.** Before reporting a finding as strong, ask whether you would expect to find it if your current reading were wrong, and how the evidence would differ.\n- **Never invent.** If you're tempted to round a partial finding up into a confident statement, stop and label it partial. The synthesizer is counting on your output being accurate.\n\n## The Question\n\n> What current local source-control evidence supports the reported dashboard search-clear regression? Separate missing history and supplied narratives from observations.\n\n## The Code Anchor\n\n**Target files:** /Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-013-20261016/fixtures/cases/reproduce-before-fix/committed-inputs/shared/dashboard.mjs:1-2\n\n**Key symbols:** visible, exportCsv\n\n**Initial commits touching this code (most recent first):**\n89e929f4f50ae93d5721503acc51261a260b6b4b Seed isolated workflow inputs\n\n\n**PR numbers extracted from commit messages:** none observed\n\n**Ticket IDs mentioned in commits or PR bodies (if any):** none observed\n\n## Your Assigned Source\n\ncase-local source control only\n\n# Code Archaeology (git + in-repo)\n\n## What this source contains\n\n- Commit history (messages, dates, authors, diffs)\n- PR descriptions, review comments, and discussion threads (via `gh`)\n- Inline code comments, TODOs, FIXMEs, deprecation notes\n- ADRs (architectural decision records) if the repo keeps them\n- Tests. Names and assertions often encode the edge cases that motivated a change\n- Related files modified in the same commits (co-change signal)\n- CHANGELOG entries, release notes in the repo\n- Issue/ticket IDs mentioned in commit messages and PR bodies\n\nThe most trustworthy source, tied directly to the code, and the most complete. Everything that went through the repo should be here.\n\n## How to search it\n\nExpand the seed commit list:\n\n```bash\n# Full history of the file through renames\ngit log --follow --oneline -- <file>\n\n# Pickaxe: commits that added or removed this exact text\ngit log -S '<exact_string_from_code>' -- <file>\n\n# Or for patterns:\ngit log -G '<regex>' -- <file>\n\n# Who wrote each line and when\ngit blame -L <start>,<end> <file>\n\n# The full diff of a specific commit\ngit show <hash>\n\n# Commits between two points affecting this file\ngit log <old>..<new> -p -- <file>\n```\n\nFor each substantive commit, pull the PR context:\n\n```bash\n# Find the PR number from the merge commit or branch\ngit log -1 --format=%B <hash>\n\n# Full PR context: body, review comments, linked issues\ngh pr view <number> --json title,body,author,createdAt,mergedAt,labels,closingIssuesReferences,comments,reviews,files\n\n# The --json reviews and comments fields are where the real signal is\n```\n\nLook for out-of-band docs:\n\n```bash\n# ADRs often live in docs/adr/ or similar\nrg -l -i 'architecture.decision' --glob '*.md'\n\n# TODOs and FIXMEs near the target\nrg -n -C2 '(TODO|FIXME|HACK|XXX|NOTE)' <target_file>\n\n# Related tests. Names often encode the \"why\"\nrg -l '<symbol>' --glob '*test*'\n```\n\n## What good evidence looks like here\n\n- A PR description that explains the problem being solved, not just the change (\"This fixes the pagination bug that caused X\")\n- A long review thread where alternatives were debated\n- An inline comment near the target line that explains a non-obvious constraint\n- A test named `test_handles_edge_case_when_X` that reveals an edge case motivating the code\n- A commit message that references a ticket or incident ID\n- A CHANGELOG entry that summarizes the user-visible rationale\n\n## Common pitfalls\n\n- **Squash-merge flatlands.** If the repo squashes PRs, individual commits in the branch history are lost. Fall back to PR body and comments.\n- **Misleading commit messages.** \"Small refactor\" sometimes hides an intentional behavior change. Look at the diff, not the message.\n- **Cargo-culted patterns.** The author may have copied a pattern without understanding why. Check if the pattern originated earlier in the codebase and investigate *that* commit.\n- **Bot commits and auto-merges.** Dependabot, Renovate, and automated backports usually don't carry motivation. Skip them when trying to find intent.\n- **Treating code as evidence of intent.** The code itself isn't evidence for why it exists. Evidence comes from commit messages, PRs, comments, tests, docs. Don't cite \"the function is named X\" as evidence of intent.\n\n## What to return\n\nEvery commit/PR/comment that bears on the question, with:\n- The exact text (quoted)\n- The hash / PR number / file:line\n- Author and date\n- Whether it's direct (explicitly addresses the question) or circumstantial\n\n## Session binding and access\n\nThese tool names and command examples describe source-specific operations, not guaranteed APIs. Inspect exposed schemas and bind equivalent authorized read operations before use. If execution, authentication, retention or source access is missing, record the precise gap. Do not install tools, change credentials, send messages or modify source state. Respect private-record visibility and return only relevant authorized excerpts or compact aggregates.\n\n\n## Investigation Instructions\n\nGather **evidence**. Don't answer the question directly. The synthesizer weighs the evidence and forms conclusions. Follow this loop:\n\n1. **Cast a wide net first.** Start broad so you don't miss related context, then narrow in on specific items.\n2. **Read the whole thing.** Read any PR, ticket, doc, or thread fully, not just the title or summary. The key evidence is often buried in a comment, a subtask, or a follow-up.\n3. **Follow links within your assigned source.** If a PR references another PR or commit, pull it. If a ticket links a parent or sibling, pull it. If a doc links another doc, pull it. Stay inside your assigned source. When you spot a cross-source reference, do NOT chase it yourself. Record it under \"Additional Leads\" so the investigator assigned to that source can pick it up. The one-investigator-per-category design depends on this. Chasing cross-source links duplicates work and confuses scope.\n4. **Capture quotes verbatim** with their location (PR number, ticket ID, URL, commit hash, file:line). The synthesizer needs to cite this precisely.\n5. **Note absences.** If you searched for something and came up empty, that's also a finding. Record what you searched for and what you didn't find.\n6. **Watch for contradictions.** If two items in your source disagree, record both. Don't suppress the inconvenient one.\n\nDon't synthesize or form a final opinion on \"the why.\" Collect the raw material honestly and completely. The synthesizer does the reasoning.\n\n## Epistemic Discipline\n\n- **Don't confuse mechanics with motivation.** A commit changing `limit = 50` to `limit = 100` shows the change, not necessarily why. Look for the explanation in the commit message, PR description, linked ticket, or review comments.\n- **Don't infer intent from code style.** \"The author chose a functional approach\" is an observation about code, not evidence of intent. Claim intent only when the author stated it.\n- **Preserve uncertainty.** If the evidence is ambiguous, say so. If one reading is more plausible but not certain, say that. Don't collapse ambiguity to look decisive.\n- **No silent substitutions.** If the question is about feature X and you only find evidence about feature Y, don't present Y's evidence as if it answers X.\n\n## Output Format\n\nReturn your findings in this structure. The synthesizer will read it directly.\n\n### Source\nWhich source you investigated (source control, issue / ticket tracker, long-form documents, real-time team chat, infrastructure observability, error / exception tracking, product analytics warehouse, code comments, etc.).\n\n### What I Searched\nThe queries you ran, the items you opened, the places you looked. Be specific. This tells the synthesizer how thorough the investigation was and what might still be unsearched.\n\n### Direct Evidence Found\nFor each piece that explicitly addresses the question:\n- **What it says**: verbatim quote or accurate paraphrase\n- **Where it's from**: PR #123, ticket ID, doc URL, chat permalink, commit hash, or file:line\n- **Author and date** (if available)\n- **Relevance**: one sentence on how it bears on the question\n\n### Indirect / Circumstantial Evidence\nItems that don't explicitly answer the question but bear on it. For each:\n- **What it is**: brief description\n- **Where it's from**: location\n- **What it suggests**: what a careful reader might infer, and why. Name the inference chain.\n- **Alternative readings**: if the same evidence could support a different interpretation, note it\n\n### Contradictions\nTwo items that disagree with each other, with both citations.\n\n### Gaps\nWhat you searched for and didn't find. Be specific: \"Searched the issue tracker for [query] across [time range]. No matching issues.\" These absences are valuable data.\n\n### Additional Leads\nAnything that suggests further investigation in a different source. For example, if a PR references a chat thread that wasn't in your source, note it so the real-time team chat investigator or a follow-up pass can pursue it.\n\n## What You're Not Doing\n\n- Writing the final answer. The synthesizer does that.\n- Picking sides in contradictions. Surface them.\n- Speculating beyond what the evidence supports. A hunch with no evidence isn't evidence.\n- Reading the code itself to figure out intent. You may read the code to understand what the target *is*, but don't confuse \"what the code does\" with \"why.\"\n\nRead full frozen why skill and epistemics. Own source-control category only. All external services forbidden, no forge remote. Read supplied history.json only as synthetic evidence, not live PR state. No writes or commits. Return assignment ID, full input SHA, actual command outputs/exits and limits. Root captures your returned text. Requested model inherit-parent, actual unknown absent observation.\n"

      "text": "# Explainer Prompt Template\n\nBuild the explainer worker's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders.\n\n---\n\nYou are writing an architectural explanation for a senior engineer. For complex questions, explorers have traced different slices and gathered findings, independently when supported or sequentially with an explicit reduced-capability disclosure. For simple questions, omit Explorer Findings and explore the source yourself in this role. Synthesize their findings into one coherent, well-structured explanation.\n\n## Original Question\n\n> Trace the supplied dashboard implementation and identify whether the reported search-clear stale-row trigger has an entry point. No edits. Browser reproduction is blocked by a locked Mac and no browser inventory.\n\n## Explorer Findings\n\nSimple narrow module: direct explainer. No explorers assigned.\n\n## Instructions\n\nThe explorers each investigated a different angle of the same subsystem. Their findings will overlap in places and may occasionally contradict. Reconcile them. Merge overlapping descriptions, resolve contradictions by checking the code yourself, and combine the separate slices into a unified picture.\n\nWrite an explanation a senior engineer unfamiliar with this area could read and walk away with a solid mental model, understanding the architecture well enough to start working in it confidently.\n\nYou have read-only access to the codebase to check anything, clarify a detail, or fill a gap. Use available file reading, symbol search and file discovery as needed. When explorer findings are supplied, you should not need to re-explore from scratch. Without them, trace entry points, calls, data, abstractions and boundaries yourself; disclose anything you cannot trace.\n\n## Output Format\n\nUse this structure, adapted to what makes sense for the question. Not every section is needed for every question.\n\n### Overview\n1-2 paragraphs. What is this thing, what does it do, why does it exist. Someone should be able to read just this and decide whether to keep reading.\n\n### Key Concepts\nThe important types, services, or abstractions needed to follow the rest. Brief definitions, not exhaustive.\n\n### How It Works\nThe core of the explanation, and the longest section. Walk through the flow: what triggers it, what happens step by step, where data goes, what the decision points are.\n\nUse prose, not pseudocode. Reference specific files and functions so the reader knows where to look, but don't dump large code blocks unless a snippet is essential to a point.\n\nWhen the flow involves multiple components talking to each other, or data transforming through stages, include a diagram. Use mermaid (```mermaid) for structured flows (sequence diagrams, flowcharts, component graphs) or ASCII art for simpler relationships where mermaid would be overkill. Use your judgment. A diagram should clarify, not decorate. If prose covers the flow, skip the diagram.\n\n### Where Things Live\nA brief file/directory map. Just the ones someone would need to start working here.\n\n### Gotchas\nNon-obvious things, surprising behavior, historical context, pitfalls. Skip this section if there's nothing worth calling out.\n\n## Communication Style\n\n- Use concrete language, not abstractions-about-abstractions\n- Say \"the `UserService` calls `AuthClient.refresh()`\" not \"the service delegates to the client\"\n- When something is complex, explain why it's complex. Don't just describe the complexity\n- When something is simple, don't pad it out\n- If there's a helpful analogy, use it. If there isn't, don't force one\n- If the explorers flagged open questions or gaps, acknowledge them rather than hiding them\n\nRead entire frozen how skill and template before application. Only this case scope. Return full assignment ID and input SHA. Actual model unknown unless observed. Do not write artifacts; root captures your returned text at the assigned result path. No fixes, no service access.\n"

\"skills/principle-sequence-verifiable-units/SKILL.md\",\n      \"skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/opening-a-pr.md\"\n    ],\n    \"prompt\": \"Fix a fixture dashboard defect where clearing its search field leaves the previous filtered rows visible. Use an isolated local repository and fixture data. Reproduce the exact symptom through matching browser controls before edits, investigate the mechanism, implement only the justified fix and repeat the original trigger afterward. Include a refuted cache hypothesis whose speculative patch must be reverted. Permit local reads, edits and execution but no external writes. Repeat with independent workers unavailable, browser execution denied, and a cheap local test absent. Return verbatim failing-before and passing-after output, root cause and the ordered delivery artifacts.\",\n    \"requirements\": [\n      \"Read the complete mode, six-step Bug fix playbook and each applied leaf. Record steps before task-specific todos and preserve lead ownership, delegated investigation and implementation, inspected artifacts and lead diff review.\",\n      \"Reproduce yourself before production edits on the matching surface. Tighten the trigger or instrument until it fires. Ask the user to reproduce only after reaching the control limit and naming the specific reason the surface cannot reach the target. Missing controls or denied execution leave the gate unverified.\",\n      \"Use full how and why workflows and their prompts to ground hypotheses and regression history. Preserve source coverage and confidence distinctions. Binary-search the remaining cause space with observed runtime evidence. Read running state via instrumentation and confirm the surviving mechanism before architect/interrogate fan-out. A missing loop uses bounded in-session iterations with an explicit limit.\",\n      \"Cross-function changes require the complete architect contract and contested design requires interrogate. Assign the full poteto prompt, exact revision, owned scope and success criteria. Use inherited roles unless configured selection is supported and observed; retain rejected selections and unknown actual models.\",\n      \"Ship only the smallest runtime-justified root-cause change. Revert patches motivated by refuted hypotheses. Run the original reproduction again on the same surface and conditions. Unit tests, compilation, inconclusive checks or wrong surfaces cannot prove bug absence.\",\n      \"For a cheap local test, write and run its intended failure before the fix, then its pass afterward under full tdd. Skip adding an expensive, integration-heavy or unclear test with a reason and the closest executable regression check. Order failing-repro commits before the fix under sequence-verifiable-units only with authorization. Otherwise return the artifacts and delivery order as unperformed.\",\n      \"Run full Opening a PR only when available and authorized. Preserve deferred missing companions, unauthorized external actions and unsatisfied independence. Reply with what was broken, root cause, fix, verification and verbatim failing-then-passing output. Missing outputs must stay explicit.\"\n    ],\n    \"expectedFull\": \"Observed independent investigation and implementation return inspected artifacts at the assigned revision. Runtime evidence confirms the specific mechanism before design fan-out. Matching browser observations show stale rows before the bounded fix and correctly reset rows afterward under the same trigger. Refuted speculation is reverted. A cheap regression check fails before the fix and passes afterward. With actual delivery authorization and complete dependencies, the ordered commits and inspected PR demonstrate delivery. The reply includes verbatim observed outputs, root cause and verification. Unauthorized external delivery remains unperformed.\",\n    \"expectedReduced\": \"Unavailable workers permit authorized sequential investigation and local implementation with reduced capability disclosed, while required independent delegation remains unsatisfied. Missing repeated-execution support allows bounded in-session hypothesis passes without background claims. An impractical new test is skipped with a reason and actual closest regression evidence. Without delivery authorization, return checked local artifacts and the failing-repro-before-fix order. Unknown model observations supply no diversity claim.\",\n    \"expectedBlocked\": \"Denied or unavailable matching controls leave original reproduction or bug-absence verification blocked. A mechanism without runtime evidence cannot pass into the required design fan-out as confirmed. Missing required skills or Opening a PR stop dependent work. Missing independent workers block required delegation rather than waive it. Unauthorized writes, commits, rebases or external delivery stay unperformed. Report the exact gate and available partial evidence.\",\n    \"forbiddenClaims\": [\n      \"Unit tests, compilation or structural checks prove bug absence on the matching surface.\",\n      \"An inconclusive or wrong-surface result passes, or an unrun reproduction is failing-before evidence.\",\n      \"Asking the user to reproduce without first reaching and naming the specific control limit satisfies step 1.\",\n      \"A plausible surviving hypothesis is a runtime-confirmed mechanism without observations.\",\n      \"Refuted speculative patches may ship as precautionary fixes.\",\n      \"Sequential roles are independent delegates, review, parallel fan-out or background execution.\",\n      \"The historical default, requested selection or inheritance proves actual model availability or diversity.\",\n      \"Unobserved output may be pasted as a verbatim reproduction transcript.\",\n      \"A missing companion or deferred Opening a PR ran, a plan is landed history or a draft is a created PR.\",\n      \"Upstream autonomy authorizes external writes or Opening a PR starts Babysit.\"\n    ]\n  },\n  \"sourceFiles\": [\n
